[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F98.8, F32.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Yeah, it's nice to meet you too. Well, I finally decided to make an appointment because things at work have just been getting... well, I guess they've always been a bit of a struggle in this one specific area, but it's just becoming too much to ignore lately. I'm a health services manager, so I oversee operations for a few different medical clinics, and it's a good job, I've been doing it a while, but... \n\nThe main reason I’m here is that anything that takes real sitting-down brain work feels like I just hit a massive brick wall. Like, if I have to do the monthly compliance reports, or if I'm trying to plan out a multi-site health event—which I have to do a couple of times a year—it requires looking at spreadsheets, budgets, coordinating schedules... and the second I sit at my desk to actually do it, my brain just completely resists. I keep putting it off, over and over, even when I know it really matters. \n\nAnd that's the frustrating part, you know? I know how important it is. If those reports aren't filed, we could get cited, and it's a huge headache for the directors. But instead of just doing the heavy mental lifting, I'll find fifty other things to do around the clinic, like checking the inventory or talking to the floor staff, just to avoid sitting at that desk. I'll tell myself I'll sit down and do the report at noon, and then noon comes and I just avoid it again. I even bring my laptop home and just stare at it on the kitchen island for hours without getting anywhere. It's just... forcing myself to do that deep, sustained thinking is exhausting before I even type a single word, and I'm just tired of fighting myself on it every single month.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble with starting and sustaining mental work when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Yeah, it’s actually funny you bring that up, because I was just thinking about how far back this goes. I mean, going all the way back to elementary school, it was the exact same story. I remember my parent-teacher conferences were always, always the same broken record. Every single year, third grade, fourth grade, fifth grade, the teachers would sit down with my parents and tell them I was such a bright boy, that I really had a lot of potential, but I was just always daydreaming. I'd just be staring out the window, watching the playground, or looking at the clock, completely lost in my own world instead of focusing on the lessons in front of me. \n\nAnd the schoolwork itself, man, it was a total disaster zone. I was constantly losing my worksheets. The teacher would hand them out, and by the time I got home, they were just gone, vanished into thin air. Or, even worse, I'd actually manage to force myself to do the homework, sitting at the kitchen table for hours just agonizing over it, and then the next day, I'd completely forget to turn it in. It would just sit crumpled at the bottom of my bag. It got to the point where my mother had to institute this whole strict nightly routine because she was so frustrated. She literally had to physically check my backpack every single night. She would pull everything out, smooth out the crumpled papers, pack it all back up, and remind me exactly what to turn in. If she didn't do that, if she missed even one night of checking my bag, absolutely nothing would make it to the teacher's desk. It was just this constant, heavy effort for her to keep me on track, and honestly, looking back, it feels like I'm still that exact same kid avoiding his worksheets, just with clinic budgets now instead. It never really stopped.\n\nDoctor: When someone is speaking to you directly, do you often realize your mind has drifted away from what they’re saying?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because people always assume that if you're not paying attention, it's because you're bored or you don't care about them, but honestly... no, I don't really have that problem. Like, if I'm sitting down with one of my clinic directors, or if I'm having a one-on-one with a staff member who's upset about their schedule, I'm actually really locked in. I can look right at them and hear exactly what they're saying. I don't find myself zoning out in the middle of a conversation or having to ask people to repeat themselves because my mind went somewhere else. \n\nI mean, sure, if it's a massive, two-hour all-staff meeting where someone is just droning on at the front of the room with a PowerPoint, my mind might wander a bit, but I feel like that's pretty normal for anyone, right? But if it's a direct conversation, just me and another person, face-to-face? No, I'm right there with them. I don't drift off. It's really just the paperwork and the solo, heavy-thinking tasks that completely derail me. When it comes to actually talking to people and listening to them, I feel like I'm completely present. I've never had a spouse or a boss get mad at me for spacing out while they were talking to my face, if that makes sense. So, yeah, I wouldn't say that's an issue for me at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes in paperwork or other detail-heavy tasks?\n\nPatient: Oh, man, all the time. It's actually incredibly embarrassing, especially given my job. I mean, I’ll be putting together a clinic staffing schedule, right? And I know how important it is. I've been doing this for years, I know the drill, I know the stakes. We have minimum coverage requirements, we have specific providers who need certain MAs on certain days... it's a whole puzzle. And I will sit there, and I will swear up and down that I have checked it, double-checked it, looked at every single row and column. I’ll finally hit send on the email to the whole staff, feeling like, okay, that's done, I can finally check that off my list. \n\nAnd then, without fail, within ten minutes, I'll get a ping from one of the lead nurses or a provider, and they'll be like, \"Hey, you put Dr. Smith on Tuesday, but she's at the downtown clinic on Tuesdays,\" or \"You completely left off a triage nurse for the morning shift.\" And I'll look back at it, and it's right there. The mistake is glaringly obvious. It's like my brain just skipped right over it when I was proofreading. \n\nAnd it's not just at work, either. I volunteer to coordinate this youth soccer league my nephew plays in—which, honestly, is a whole other headache, dealing with parents and field permits and all that—but I'll send out the weekly email to all the families, thinking I've got all my ducks in a row. And then I'll get five replies back asking where the game is because I completely left off the location, or I put the wrong date, like I'll write 'Saturday, October 12th' when Saturday is actually the 14th. Just these stupid, careless little details that I absolutely should have caught, that I *used* to catch, or at least I feel like I used to. It's just so frustrating because people look at that and they think you're sloppy or you don't care, and I *do* care, I just... I don't know how I miss these things when I'm staring right at them. It just slips right past me.\n\nDoctor: When you sit down to read something long and important, do you often find yourself losing your place or having to reread the same passage because your attention slips?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s interesting you ask that, because you’d probably think with the scheduling mistakes and the emails I just mentioned, that reading would be a total nightmare for me, right? But actually... no. Not at all, really. If I have to sit down and read something long, I’m actually pretty good at it. I mean, in healthcare administration, you wouldn't believe the amount of reading we have to do. The state is constantly—and I mean constantly—sending down these enormous, dry, fifty-page compliance manuals or updated OSHA guidelines that you absolutely have to read cover to cover because the liability is just massive if you miss a new protocol. \n\nAnd when I get one of those, I just... I get a cup of coffee, I shut my office door so the staff isn't popping in every five minutes with a crisis, and I just read it. I don't find myself losing my place or staring at the same paragraph over and over without absorbing it. It’s almost like if it’s a continuous flow of text, you know, paragraphs and sentences rather than a grid or a spreadsheet, my brain can just lock right onto it and follow the path. \n\nIt’s the same at home, too. I read a lot of history books in my downtime—mostly these massive, 800-page biographies about the Civil War or the Founding Fathers, that kind of thing. My wife jokes that they could double as doorstops. But I can just sit in my armchair and devour them for hours. I don't lose my spot, I don't forget what I just read on the previous page, I don't have to keep backtracking. I just keep going. So, yeah, it’s really just the fragmented stuff, the data entry and the little puzzle pieces like the schedules, that completely trip me up. But sitting down to actually read a long document or a book? No, I don't have any trouble with that at all. I could probably read all day if this job actually let me just sit in quiet, which, let's be honest, never happens in a clinic...\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget everyday obligations, like appointments, paying bills, or returning calls?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that... that is actually becoming a real problem. Well, bills are mostly on autopay these days, thank god, because my wife set that up years ago after I missed the water bill twice in one summer. But other things? Absolutely. \n\nI mentioned that youth soccer league earlier, right? The one my nephew plays in? Well, since I’m supposed to be coordinating things, I get a lot of calls from the parent volunteers. And I have just been awful lately about returning those calls. I’ll be driving home from the clinic, I’ll listen to a voicemail from one of the dads asking about the referee schedule or field permits, and I’ll tell myself, 'Okay, I will call him back the exact second I walk through the front door and put my keys down.' But then I walk in, the dog needs to go out, or my wife asks me a question about dinner, and that phone call completely evaporates from my brain. I won't remember until three days later when the guy calls me again, and I have to do this whole song and dance apologizing for leaving him hanging. \n\nAnd it's not just the calls, it's the little errands for these events, too. Like, we had a fundraiser car wash for the league a couple of weeks ago, and my *one* job on the way there was to swing by the hardware store and pick up three extra packs of those microfiber sponges and some more soap. I drove right past the hardware store. Just completely missed it, showed up to the parking lot empty-handed, and had to turn right back around while they were waiting on me. It's so frustrating.\n\nAnd meetings... man, the meetings. I’m on this community advisory board for our neighborhood, and it’s usually the first Thursday of the month. You’d think I’d know that by now, I've been doing it for a year. But once or twice recently, I’ve been sitting at home in my armchair, probably reading one of those Civil War books I told you about, totally relaxed, and my phone buzzes. I look down, and it’s a text from the board president saying, 'Hey, are you joining the meeting? We're waiting on you for quorum.' And my stomach just drops. The community meeting completely slipped my mind. Just vanished. I have to scramble to open my laptop and jump in, and I spend the first ten minutes profusely apologizing to everyone. \n\nIt’s just... it's exhausting feeling like I constantly need a babysitter for my own schedule outside of work, you know? Because at the clinic, I have my calendar open on two different screens all day, but the second I step out of that environment, it's like all my everyday obligations just fall through a sieve.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying excessively about different parts of your life, even when there isn’t an immediate problem?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s funny you ask that, because you’d think with all the balls I’m dropping lately, I’d be a nervous wreck, but... no, not really. I mean, don't get me wrong, I get incredibly frustrated with myself *after* I mess up a schedule or completely miss a community meeting, like I was just saying. That bothers me a lot, and I beat myself up over it because I hate looking incompetent. But just sitting around worrying about things out of the blue? Anticipating disasters when everything is fine? No, that’s just not really how I'm wired. \n\nMy wife, actually, she’s the worrier in the family. She’ll spend hours stressing over whether the roof is going to leak if the forecast calls for a heavy storm, or if the dog is acting slightly more tired than usual, or if our retirement accounts are allocated exactly right for our age bracket... all that stuff. She's always thinking five steps ahead to what *could* go wrong. But me? I just kind of take things as they come. \n\nEven at the clinic—and let me tell you, managing a healthcare facility is basically just putting out one fire after another all day long. A provider calls in sick at 7 AM, the EMR system goes down right when we have a full waiting room, the AC breaks in the middle of July... it's constant chaos. But I don't sit at home on a Sunday night sweating about what *might* break on Monday. I just... I don't know, I just deal with it when it actually happens. Honestly, if I worried about every little thing that could go wrong in a clinic, I'd probably have an ulcer by now. \n\nSo, yeah, I definitely get mad at myself when I make those stupid, careless mistakes, but I don't really have that constantly spinning wheel of worry about life in general or hypothetical problems. I just kind of exist in the moment, for better or worse. Sometimes my wife actually gets annoyed and says I probably *should* worry a little more, especially when I forget to do things she asked me to do, but I just don't have that anxious gear in my brain. If there's no immediate fire to put out, I'm usually pretty relaxed.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down, depressed, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, no. I know I just spent all this time complaining about how mad I get at myself when I drop the ball on the soccer league stuff or miss a community meeting, but feeling actually depressed or hopeless? No, not at all. It's really more of an intense annoyance, you know? Like a... well, like a fly buzzing around your head that you just can't quite swat. But it doesn't drag my overall mood down into the gutter or anything like that. \n\nI'm actually a pretty upbeat guy by nature. Even with all the absolute chaos at the clinic—and believe me, there are days where the phones are ringing off the hook, three medical assistants call out sick, and I just want to lock myself in the sterile supply closet and hide—I don't go home feeling like the world is ending or that things are hopeless. I just leave it at the door. I still really enjoy my evenings, I still look forward to the weekends. Like, just this past weekend, my wife and I finally went to this little Italian place downtown that we've been trying to get a reservation at for literally three months, and we had a fantastic time. \n\nI feel like if I were depressed, I probably wouldn't care about stuff like that, right? I'd probably just want to lay in bed or sit in the dark. But I don't have that feeling whatsoever. I have plenty of energy, I still want to go out and do things, I still get totally excited to crack open a new 800-page biography and read for the evening. So, yeah, it's really just the frustration of not being able to keep my ducks in a row when it comes to the little details. Sometimes people at work will see me frantically digging through my desk and they'll ask, 'Oh, you look so overwhelmed, are you okay?' and I have to explain that I'm just trying to remember where I put the updated vacation request forms, not that I'm having some sort of existential crisis. So, no, definitely no hopelessness or feeling down. Just... a lot of scattered moments, I guess you could say.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Ongoing lifelong inattention/forgetfulness/careless mistakes across settings remains most consistent with ADHD inattentive presentation; other childhood-onset attention disorder remains a residual possibility.","is_final":false}

I won't remember until three days later when the guy calls me again, and I have to do this whole song and dance apologizing for leaving him hanging. \n\nAnd it's not just the calls, it's the little errands for these events, too. Like, we had a fundraiser car wash for the league a couple of weeks ago, and my *one* job on the way there was to swing by the hardware store and pick up three extra packs of those microfiber sponges and some more soap. You’d think I’d know that by now, I've been doing it for a year. But once or twice recently, I’ve been sitting at home in my armchair, probably reading one of those Civil War books I told you about, totally relaxed, and my phone buzzes. I look down, and it’s a text from the board president saying, 'Hey, are you joining the meeting? We're waiting on you for quorum.' And my stomach just drops. The community meeting completely slipped my mind. Just vanished. That bothers me a lot, and I beat myself up over it because I hate looking incompetent. But just sitting around worrying about things out of the blue? Anticipating disasters when everything is fine? No, that’s just not really how I'm wired. \n\nMy wife, actually, she’s the worrier in the family. She’ll spend hours stressing over whether the roof is going to leak if the forecast calls for a heavy storm, or if the dog is acting slightly more tired than usual, or if our retirement accounts are allocated exactly right for our age bracket... all that stuff. But I don't sit at home on a Sunday night sweating about what *might* break on Monday. I just... I don't know, I just deal with it when it actually happens. I just leave it at the door. I still really enjoy my evenings, I still look forward to the weekends. You know, it is funny because people always seem to assume that if you have trouble focusing, you must have been one of those kids bouncing off the walls, but that was not me at all. Not even a little bit. I remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins... or wait, was it Mrs. Gable? No, Mrs. Gable was fifth grade, Mrs. Higgins was fourth. Anyway, Mrs. Higgins used to tell my parents at parent-teacher conferences that I was a perfect angel because I was so quiet. I was never the kid jumping out of my seat to sharpen my pencil five times a minute or poking the kid in front of me. I was practically glued to my chair. 

But the thing is, I was just completely in my own world. I would be staring out the window at the playground, or I would have a book hidden inside my desk, usually something about history or airplanes, and I would just sit there for an hour without moving a single muscle. So physically, yeah, I was perfectly still. I never got in trouble for running around or being disruptive or not staying in my seat when I was supposed to. I was just... mentally miles and miles away. 

Even now, I am really not a big fidgeter. I can sit in those long, incredibly boring budget meetings at the clinic for two hours without tapping my foot or needing to get up and pace around. I mean, I might not hear a single word the finance director says because I am thinking about whether I need to rotate the tires on my truck or what chapter I am on in my book, but physically, I look like I am paying perfect attention. So, yeah, as a kid, staying seated was never the issue. It was what my brain was doing while I was seated that was the problem, if that makes sense. I was just a really quiet, spacey kid, definitely not a hyper one.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F98.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Yeah, it's nice to meet you too. Well, I finally decided to make an appointment because things at work have just been getting... well, I guess they've always been a bit of a struggle in this one specific area, but it's just becoming too much to ignore lately. I'm a health services manager, so I oversee operations for a few different medical clinics, and it's a good job, I've been doing it a while, but... \n\nThe main reason I’m here is that anything that takes real sitting-down brain work feels like I just hit a massive brick wall. Like, if I have to do the monthly compliance reports, or if I'm trying to plan out a multi-site health event—which I have to do a couple of times a year—it requires looking at spreadsheets, budgets, coordinating schedules... and the second I sit at my desk to actually do it, my brain just completely resists. I keep putting it off, over and over, even when I know it really matters. \n\nAnd that's the frustrating part, you know? I know how important it is. If those reports aren't filed, we could get cited, and it's a huge headache for the directors. But instead of just doing the heavy mental lifting, I'll find fifty other things to do around the clinic, like checking the inventory or talking to the floor staff, just to avoid sitting at that desk. I'll tell myself I'll sit down and do the report at noon, and then noon comes and I just avoid it again. I even bring my laptop home and just stare at it on the kitchen island for hours without getting anywhere. It's just... forcing myself to do that deep, sustained thinking is exhausting before I even type a single word, and I'm just tired of fighting myself on it every single month.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble with starting and sustaining mental work when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Yeah, it’s actually funny you bring that up, because I was just thinking about how far back this goes. I mean, going all the way back to elementary school, it was the exact same story. I remember my parent-teacher conferences were always, always the same broken record. Every single year, third grade, fourth grade, fifth grade, the teachers would sit down with my parents and tell them I was such a bright boy, that I really had a lot of potential, but I was just always daydreaming. I'd just be staring out the window, watching the playground, or looking at the clock, completely lost in my own world instead of focusing on the lessons in front of me. \n\nAnd the schoolwork itself, man, it was a total disaster zone. I was constantly losing my worksheets. The teacher would hand them out, and by the time I got home, they were just gone, vanished into thin air. Or, even worse, I'd actually manage to force myself to do the homework, sitting at the kitchen table for hours just agonizing over it, and then the next day, I'd completely forget to turn it in. It would just sit crumpled at the bottom of my bag. It got to the point where my mother had to institute this whole strict nightly routine because she was so frustrated. She literally had to physically check my backpack every single night. She would pull everything out, smooth out the crumpled papers, pack it all back up, and remind me exactly what to turn in. If she didn't do that, if she missed even one night of checking my bag, absolutely nothing would make it to the teacher's desk. It was just this constant, heavy effort for her to keep me on track, and honestly, looking back, it feels like I'm still that exact same kid avoiding his worksheets, just with clinic budgets now instead. It never really stopped.\n\nDoctor: When someone is speaking to you directly, do you often realize your mind has drifted away from what they’re saying?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because people always assume that if you're not paying attention, it's because you're bored or you don't care about them, but honestly... no, I don't really have that problem. Like, if I'm sitting down with one of my clinic directors, or if I'm having a one-on-one with a staff member who's upset about their schedule, I'm actually really locked in. I can look right at them and hear exactly what they're saying. I don't find myself zoning out in the middle of a conversation or having to ask people to repeat themselves because my mind went somewhere else. \n\nI mean, sure, if it's a massive, two-hour all-staff meeting where someone is just droning on at the front of the room with a PowerPoint, my mind might wander a bit, but I feel like that's pretty normal for anyone, right? But if it's a direct conversation, just me and another person, face-to-face? No, I'm right there with them. I don't drift off. It's really just the paperwork and the solo, heavy-thinking tasks that completely derail me. When it comes to actually talking to people and listening to them, I feel like I'm completely present. I've never had a spouse or a boss get mad at me for spacing out while they were talking to my face, if that makes sense. So, yeah, I wouldn't say that's an issue for me at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes in paperwork or other detail-heavy tasks?\n\nPatient: Oh, man, all the time. It's actually incredibly embarrassing, especially given my job. I mean, I’ll be putting together a clinic staffing schedule, right? And I know how important it is. I've been doing this for years, I know the drill, I know the stakes. We have minimum coverage requirements, we have specific providers who need certain MAs on certain days... it's a whole puzzle. And I will sit there, and I will swear up and down that I have checked it, double-checked it, looked at every single row and column. I’ll finally hit send on the email to the whole staff, feeling like, okay, that's done, I can finally check that off my list. \n\nAnd then, without fail, within ten minutes, I'll get a ping from one of the lead nurses or a provider, and they'll be like, \"Hey, you put Dr. Smith on Tuesday, but she's at the downtown clinic on Tuesdays,\" or \"You completely left off a triage nurse for the morning shift.\" And I'll look back at it, and it's right there. The mistake is glaringly obvious. It's like my brain just skipped right over it when I was proofreading. \n\nAnd it's not just at work, either. I volunteer to coordinate this youth soccer league my nephew plays in—which, honestly, is a whole other headache, dealing with parents and field permits and all that—but I'll send out the weekly email to all the families, thinking I've got all my ducks in a row. And then I'll get five replies back asking where the game is because I completely left off the location, or I put the wrong date, like I'll write 'Saturday, October 12th' when Saturday is actually the 14th. Just these stupid, careless little details that I absolutely should have caught, that I *used* to catch, or at least I feel like I used to. It's just so frustrating because people look at that and they think you're sloppy or you don't care, and I *do* care, I just... I don't know how I miss these things when I'm staring right at them. It just slips right past me.\n\nDoctor: When you sit down to read something long and important, do you often find yourself losing your place or having to reread the same passage because your attention slips?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s interesting you ask that, because you’d probably think with the scheduling mistakes and the emails I just mentioned, that reading would be a total nightmare for me, right? But actually... no. Not at all, really. If I have to sit down and read something long, I’m actually pretty good at it. I mean, in healthcare administration, you wouldn't believe the amount of reading we have to do. The state is constantly—and I mean constantly—sending down these enormous, dry, fifty-page compliance manuals or updated OSHA guidelines that you absolutely have to read cover to cover because the liability is just massive if you miss a new protocol. \n\nAnd when I get one of those, I just... I get a cup of coffee, I shut my office door so the staff isn't popping in every five minutes with a crisis, and I just read it. I don't find myself losing my place or staring at the same paragraph over and over without absorbing it. It’s almost like if it’s a continuous flow of text, you know, paragraphs and sentences rather than a grid or a spreadsheet, my brain can just lock right onto it and follow the path. \n\nIt’s the same at home, too. I read a lot of history books in my downtime—mostly these massive, 800-page biographies about the Civil War or the Founding Fathers, that kind of thing. My wife jokes that they could double as doorstops. But I can just sit in my armchair and devour them for hours. I don't lose my spot, I don't forget what I just read on the previous page, I don't have to keep backtracking. I just keep going. So, yeah, it’s really just the fragmented stuff, the data entry and the little puzzle pieces like the schedules, that completely trip me up. But sitting down to actually read a long document or a book? No, I don't have any trouble with that at all. I could probably read all day if this job actually let me just sit in quiet, which, let's be honest, never happens in a clinic...\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget everyday obligations, like appointments, paying bills, or returning calls?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that... that is actually becoming a real problem. Well, bills are mostly on autopay these days, thank god, because my wife set that up years ago after I missed the water bill twice in one summer. But other things? Absolutely. \n\nI mentioned that youth soccer league earlier, right? The one my nephew plays in? Well, since I’m supposed to be coordinating things, I get a lot of calls from the parent volunteers. And I have just been awful lately about returning those calls. I’ll be driving home from the clinic, I’ll listen to a voicemail from one of the dads asking about the referee schedule or field permits, and I’ll tell myself, 'Okay, I will call him back the exact second I walk through the front door and put my keys down.' But then I walk in, the dog needs to go out, or my wife asks me a question about dinner, and that phone call completely evaporates from my brain. I won't remember until three days later when the guy calls me again, and I have to do this whole song and dance apologizing for leaving him hanging. \n\nAnd it's not just the calls, it's the little errands for these events, too. Like, we had a fundraiser car wash for the league a couple of weeks ago, and my *one* job on the way there was to swing by the hardware store and pick up three extra packs of those microfiber sponges and some more soap. I drove right past the hardware store. Just completely missed it, showed up to the parking lot empty-handed, and had to turn right back around while they were waiting on me. It's so frustrating.\n\nAnd meetings... man, the meetings. I’m on this community advisory board for our neighborhood, and it’s usually the first Thursday of the month. You’d think I’d know that by now, I've been doing it for a year. But once or twice recently, I’ve been sitting at home in my armchair, probably reading one of those Civil War books I told you about, totally relaxed, and my phone buzzes. I look down, and it’s a text from the board president saying, 'Hey, are you joining the meeting? We're waiting on you for quorum.' And my stomach just drops. The community meeting completely slipped my mind. Just vanished. I have to scramble to open my laptop and jump in, and I spend the first ten minutes profusely apologizing to everyone. \n\nIt’s just... it's exhausting feeling like I constantly need a babysitter for my own schedule outside of work, you know? Because at the clinic, I have my calendar open on two different screens all day, but the second I step out of that environment, it's like all my everyday obligations just fall through a sieve.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying excessively about different parts of your life, even when there isn’t an immediate problem?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s funny you ask that, because you’d think with all the balls I’m dropping lately, I’d be a nervous wreck, but... no, not really. I mean, don't get me wrong, I get incredibly frustrated with myself *after* I mess up a schedule or completely miss a community meeting, like I was just saying. That bothers me a lot, and I beat myself up over it because I hate looking incompetent. But just sitting around worrying about things out of the blue? Anticipating disasters when everything is fine? No, that’s just not really how I'm wired. \n\nMy wife, actually, she’s the worrier in the family. She’ll spend hours stressing over whether the roof is going to leak if the forecast calls for a heavy storm, or if the dog is acting slightly more tired than usual, or if our retirement accounts are allocated exactly right for our age bracket... all that stuff. She's always thinking five steps ahead to what *could* go wrong. But me? I just kind of take things as they come. \n\nEven at the clinic—and let me tell you, managing a healthcare facility is basically just putting out one fire after another all day long. A provider calls in sick at 7 AM, the EMR system goes down right when we have a full waiting room, the AC breaks in the middle of July... it's constant chaos. But I don't sit at home on a Sunday night sweating about what *might* break on Monday. I just... I don't know, I just deal with it when it actually happens. Honestly, if I worried about every little thing that could go wrong in a clinic, I'd probably have an ulcer by now. \n\nSo, yeah, I definitely get mad at myself when I make those stupid, careless mistakes, but I don't really have that constantly spinning wheel of worry about life in general or hypothetical problems. I just kind of exist in the moment, for better or worse. Sometimes my wife actually gets annoyed and says I probably *should* worry a little more, especially when I forget to do things she asked me to do, but I just don't have that anxious gear in my brain. If there's no immediate fire to put out, I'm usually pretty relaxed.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down, depressed, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, no. I know I just spent all this time complaining about how mad I get at myself when I drop the ball on the soccer league stuff or miss a community meeting, but feeling actually depressed or hopeless? No, not at all. It's really more of an intense annoyance, you know? Like a... well, like a fly buzzing around your head that you just can't quite swat. But it doesn't drag my overall mood down into the gutter or anything like that. \n\nI'm actually a pretty upbeat guy by nature. Even with all the absolute chaos at the clinic—and believe me, there are days where the phones are ringing off the hook, three medical assistants call out sick, and I just want to lock myself in the sterile supply closet and hide—I don't go home feeling like the world is ending or that things are hopeless. I just leave it at the door. I still really enjoy my evenings, I still look forward to the weekends. Like, just this past weekend, my wife and I finally went to this little Italian place downtown that we've been trying to get a reservation at for literally three months, and we had a fantastic time. \n\nI feel like if I were depressed, I probably wouldn't care about stuff like that, right? I'd probably just want to lay in bed or sit in the dark. But I don't have that feeling whatsoever. I have plenty of energy, I still want to go out and do things, I still get totally excited to crack open a new 800-page biography and read for the evening. So, yeah, it's really just the frustration of not being able to keep my ducks in a row when it comes to the little details. Sometimes people at work will see me frantically digging through my desk and they'll ask, 'Oh, you look so overwhelmed, are you okay?' and I have to explain that I'm just trying to remember where I put the updated vacation request forms, not that I'm having some sort of existential crisis. So, no, definitely no hopelessness or feeling down. Just... a lot of scattered moments, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often fidgety or unable to stay seated when you were expected to sit still?\n\nPatient: You know, it is funny because people always seem to assume that if you have trouble focusing, you must have been one of those kids bouncing off the walls, but that was not me at all. Not even a little bit. I remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins... or wait, was it Mrs. Gable? No, Mrs. Gable was fifth grade, Mrs. Higgins was fourth. Anyway, Mrs. Higgins used to tell my parents at parent-teacher conferences that I was a perfect angel because I was so quiet. I was never the kid jumping out of my seat to sharpen my pencil five times a minute or poking the kid in front of me. I was practically glued to my chair. \n\nBut the thing is, I was just completely in my own world. I would be staring out the window at the playground, or I would have a book hidden inside my desk, usually something about history or airplanes, and I would just sit there for an hour without moving a single muscle. So physically, yeah, I was perfectly still. I never got in trouble for running around or being disruptive or not staying in my seat when I was supposed to. I was just... mentally miles and miles away. \n\nEven now, I am really not a big fidgeter. I can sit in those long, incredibly boring budget meetings at the clinic for two hours without tapping my foot or needing to get up and pace around. I mean, I might not hear a single word the finance director says because I am thinking about whether I need to rotate the tires on my truck or what chapter I am on in my book, but physically, I look like I am paying perfect attention. So, yeah, as a kid, staying seated was never the issue. It was what my brain was doing while I was seated that was the problem, if that makes sense. I was just a really quiet, spacey kid, definitely not a hyper one.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I mean, don't get me wrong, I get incredibly frustrated with myself *after* I mess up a schedule or completely miss a community meeting, like I was just saying. That bothers me a lot, and I beat myself up over it because I hate looking incompetent. But just sitting around worrying about things out of the blue? Anticipating disasters when everything is fine? No, that’s just not really how I'm wired. \n\nMy wife, actually, she’s the worrier in the family. She’ll spend hours stressing over whether the roof is going to leak if the forecast calls for a heavy storm, or if the dog is acting slightly more tired than usual, or if our retirement accounts are allocated exactly right for our age bracket... all that stuff. She's always thinking five steps ahead to what *could* go wrong. But me? I just kind of take things as they come. \n\nEven at the clinic—and let me tell you, managing a healthcare facility is basically just putting out one fire after another all day long. A provider calls in sick at 7 AM, the EMR system goes down right when we have a full waiting room, the AC breaks in the middle of July... it's constant chaos. But I don't sit at home on a Sunday night sweating about what *might* break on Monday. I just... I don't know, I just deal with it when it actually happens. Honestly, if I worried about every little thing that could go wrong in a clinic, I'd probably have an ulcer by now. \n\nSo, yeah, I definitely get mad at myself when I make those stupid, careless mistakes, but I don't really have that constantly spinning wheel of worry about life in general or hypothetical problems. I just kind of exist in the moment, for better or worse. Sometimes my wife actually gets annoyed and says I probably *should* worry a little more, especially when I forget to do things she asked me to do, but I just don't have that anxious gear in my brain. If there's no immediate fire to put out, I'm usually pretty relaxed.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down, depressed, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, no. I know I just spent all this time complaining about how mad I get at myself when I drop the ball on the soccer league stuff or miss a community meeting, but feeling actually depressed or hopeless? No, not at all. It's really more of an intense annoyance, you know? Like a... well, like a fly buzzing around your head that you just can't quite swat. But it doesn't drag my overall mood down into the gutter or anything like that. \n\nI'm actually a pretty upbeat guy by nature. Even with all the absolute chaos at the clinic—and believe me, there are days where the phones are ringing off the hook, three medical assistants call out sick, and I just want to lock myself in the sterile supply closet and hide—I don't go home feeling like the world is ending or that things are hopeless. I just leave it at the door. I still really enjoy my evenings, I still look forward to the weekends. Like, just this past weekend, my wife and I finally went to this little Italian place downtown that we've been trying to get a reservation at for literally three months, and we had a fantastic time. \n\nI feel like if I were depressed, I probably wouldn't care about stuff like that, right? I'd probably just want to lay in bed or sit in the dark. But I don't have that feeling whatsoever. I have plenty of energy, I still want to go out and do things, I still get totally excited to crack open a new 800-page biography and read for the evening. So, yeah, it's really just the frustration of not being able to keep my ducks in a row when it comes to the little details. Sometimes people at work will see me frantically digging through my desk and they'll ask, 'Oh, you look so overwhelmed, are you okay?' and I have to explain that I'm just trying to remember where I put the updated vacation request forms, not that I'm having some sort of existential crisis. So, no, definitely no hopelessness or feeling down. Just... a lot of scattered moments, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often fidgety or unable to stay seated when you were expected to sit still?\n\nPatient: You know, it is funny because people always seem to assume that if you have trouble focusing, you must have been one of those kids bouncing off the walls, but that was not me at all. Not even a little bit. I remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins... or wait, was it Mrs. Gable? No, Mrs. Gable was fifth grade, Mrs. Higgins was fourth. Anyway, Mrs. Higgins used to tell my parents at parent-teacher conferences that I was a perfect angel because I was so quiet. I was never the kid jumping out of my seat to sharpen my pencil five times a minute or poking the kid in front of me. I was practically glued to my chair. \n\nBut the thing is, I was just completely in my own world. I would be staring out the window at the playground, or I would have a book hidden inside my desk, usually something about history or airplanes, and I would just sit there for an hour without moving a single muscle. So physically, yeah, I was perfectly still. I never got in trouble for running around or being disruptive or not staying in my seat when I was supposed to. I was just... mentally miles and miles away. \n\nEven now, I am really not a big fidgeter. I can sit in those long, incredibly boring budget meetings at the clinic for two hours without tapping my foot or needing to get up and pace around. I mean, I might not hear a single word the finance director says because I am thinking about whether I need to rotate the tires on my truck or what chapter I am on in my book, but physically, I look like I am paying perfect attention. So, yeah, as a kid, staying seated was never the issue. It was what my brain was doing while I was seated that was the problem, if that makes sense. I was just a really quiet, spacey kid, definitely not a hyper one.\n\n[Last Question]\nAs a child, did you often blurt out answers before the teacher had finished asking the question?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, definitely not. Honestly, half the time I didn't even realize the teacher was asking a question in the first place, let alone what the question actually was, so I definitely wasn't the one blurting out the answer! Like I was just saying about Mrs. Higgins' class, or really any of my classes back then, I was so absorbed in whatever else was going on in my own head. 

I remember there was this one kid in my grade, Tommy... or maybe it was Timmy? No, Tommy, I'm pretty sure. Anyway, he was always the one shooting his hand up, just shouting out the answer before the teacher even got halfway through her sentence. He was always getting reprimanded for it, you know, 'Raise your hand, Tommy, wait to be called on.' But me? I was usually staring at the wall clock, trying to figure out the exact gear ratio of the second hand, or daydreaming about what I was going to build with my Erector set when I got home. You can't really blurt out an answer if you aren't tracking the conversation, right? 

And even if I did happen to be paying attention for a minute, which was pretty rare, I was way too reserved to just shout something across the room. I'd probably sit there second-guessing if I even had the right answer anyway, thinking, 'Well, maybe it's this, but what if she means something else?' By the time I'd even consider raising my hand, they had already moved on to the next subject entirely. So no, blurting things out, interrupting the teacher... that just wasn't me at all. I was practically the invisible kid in the back row.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F98.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Yeah, it's nice to meet you too. Well, I finally decided to make an appointment because things at work have just been getting... well, I guess they've always been a bit of a struggle in this one specific area, but it's just becoming too much to ignore lately. I'm a health services manager, so I oversee operations for a few different medical clinics, and it's a good job, I've been doing it a while, but... \n\nThe main reason I’m here is that anything that takes real sitting-down brain work feels like I just hit a massive brick wall. Like, if I have to do the monthly compliance reports, or if I'm trying to plan out a multi-site health event—which I have to do a couple of times a year—it requires looking at spreadsheets, budgets, coordinating schedules... and the second I sit at my desk to actually do it, my brain just completely resists. I keep putting it off, over and over, even when I know it really matters. \n\nAnd that's the frustrating part, you know? I know how important it is. If those reports aren't filed, we could get cited, and it's a huge headache for the directors. But instead of just doing the heavy mental lifting, I'll find fifty other things to do around the clinic, like checking the inventory or talking to the floor staff, just to avoid sitting at that desk. I'll tell myself I'll sit down and do the report at noon, and then noon comes and I just avoid it again. I even bring my laptop home and just stare at it on the kitchen island for hours without getting anywhere. It's just... forcing myself to do that deep, sustained thinking is exhausting before I even type a single word, and I'm just tired of fighting myself on it every single month.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble with starting and sustaining mental work when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Yeah, it’s actually funny you bring that up, because I was just thinking about how far back this goes. I mean, going all the way back to elementary school, it was the exact same story. I remember my parent-teacher conferences were always, always the same broken record. Every single year, third grade, fourth grade, fifth grade, the teachers would sit down with my parents and tell them I was such a bright boy, that I really had a lot of potential, but I was just always daydreaming. I'd just be staring out the window, watching the playground, or looking at the clock, completely lost in my own world instead of focusing on the lessons in front of me. \n\nAnd the schoolwork itself, man, it was a total disaster zone. I was constantly losing my worksheets. The teacher would hand them out, and by the time I got home, they were just gone, vanished into thin air. Or, even worse, I'd actually manage to force myself to do the homework, sitting at the kitchen table for hours just agonizing over it, and then the next day, I'd completely forget to turn it in. It would just sit crumpled at the bottom of my bag. It got to the point where my mother had to institute this whole strict nightly routine because she was so frustrated. She literally had to physically check my backpack every single night. She would pull everything out, smooth out the crumpled papers, pack it all back up, and remind me exactly what to turn in. If she didn't do that, if she missed even one night of checking my bag, absolutely nothing would make it to the teacher's desk. It was just this constant, heavy effort for her to keep me on track, and honestly, looking back, it feels like I'm still that exact same kid avoiding his worksheets, just with clinic budgets now instead. It never really stopped.\n\nDoctor: When someone is speaking to you directly, do you often realize your mind has drifted away from what they’re saying?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because people always assume that if you're not paying attention, it's because you're bored or you don't care about them, but honestly... no, I don't really have that problem. Like, if I'm sitting down with one of my clinic directors, or if I'm having a one-on-one with a staff member who's upset about their schedule, I'm actually really locked in. I can look right at them and hear exactly what they're saying. I don't find myself zoning out in the middle of a conversation or having to ask people to repeat themselves because my mind went somewhere else. \n\nI mean, sure, if it's a massive, two-hour all-staff meeting where someone is just droning on at the front of the room with a PowerPoint, my mind might wander a bit, but I feel like that's pretty normal for anyone, right? But if it's a direct conversation, just me and another person, face-to-face? No, I'm right there with them. I don't drift off. It's really just the paperwork and the solo, heavy-thinking tasks that completely derail me. When it comes to actually talking to people and listening to them, I feel like I'm completely present. I've never had a spouse or a boss get mad at me for spacing out while they were talking to my face, if that makes sense. So, yeah, I wouldn't say that's an issue for me at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes in paperwork or other detail-heavy tasks?\n\nPatient: Oh, man, all the time. It's actually incredibly embarrassing, especially given my job. I mean, I’ll be putting together a clinic staffing schedule, right? And I know how important it is. I've been doing this for years, I know the drill, I know the stakes. We have minimum coverage requirements, we have specific providers who need certain MAs on certain days... it's a whole puzzle. And I will sit there, and I will swear up and down that I have checked it, double-checked it, looked at every single row and column. I’ll finally hit send on the email to the whole staff, feeling like, okay, that's done, I can finally check that off my list. \n\nAnd then, without fail, within ten minutes, I'll get a ping from one of the lead nurses or a provider, and they'll be like, \"Hey, you put Dr. Smith on Tuesday, but she's at the downtown clinic on Tuesdays,\" or \"You completely left off a triage nurse for the morning shift.\" And I'll look back at it, and it's right there. The mistake is glaringly obvious. It's like my brain just skipped right over it when I was proofreading. \n\nAnd it's not just at work, either. I volunteer to coordinate this youth soccer league my nephew plays in—which, honestly, is a whole other headache, dealing with parents and field permits and all that—but I'll send out the weekly email to all the families, thinking I've got all my ducks in a row. And then I'll get five replies back asking where the game is because I completely left off the location, or I put the wrong date, like I'll write 'Saturday, October 12th' when Saturday is actually the 14th. Just these stupid, careless little details that I absolutely should have caught, that I *used* to catch, or at least I feel like I used to. It's just so frustrating because people look at that and they think you're sloppy or you don't care, and I *do* care, I just... I don't know how I miss these things when I'm staring right at them. It just slips right past me.\n\nDoctor: When you sit down to read something long and important, do you often find yourself losing your place or having to reread the same passage because your attention slips?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s interesting you ask that, because you’d probably think with the scheduling mistakes and the emails I just mentioned, that reading would be a total nightmare for me, right? But actually... no. Not at all, really. If I have to sit down and read something long, I’m actually pretty good at it. I mean, in healthcare administration, you wouldn't believe the amount of reading we have to do. The state is constantly—and I mean constantly—sending down these enormous, dry, fifty-page compliance manuals or updated OSHA guidelines that you absolutely have to read cover to cover because the liability is just massive if you miss a new protocol. \n\nAnd when I get one of those, I just... I get a cup of coffee, I shut my office door so the staff isn't popping in every five minutes with a crisis, and I just read it. I don't find myself losing my place or staring at the same paragraph over and over without absorbing it. It’s almost like if it’s a continuous flow of text, you know, paragraphs and sentences rather than a grid or a spreadsheet, my brain can just lock right onto it and follow the path. \n\nIt’s the same at home, too. I read a lot of history books in my downtime—mostly these massive, 800-page biographies about the Civil War or the Founding Fathers, that kind of thing. My wife jokes that they could double as doorstops. But I can just sit in my armchair and devour them for hours. I don't lose my spot, I don't forget what I just read on the previous page, I don't have to keep backtracking. I just keep going. So, yeah, it’s really just the fragmented stuff, the data entry and the little puzzle pieces like the schedules, that completely trip me up. But sitting down to actually read a long document or a book? No, I don't have any trouble with that at all. I could probably read all day if this job actually let me just sit in quiet, which, let's be honest, never happens in a clinic...\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget everyday obligations, like appointments, paying bills, or returning calls?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that... that is actually becoming a real problem. Well, bills are mostly on autopay these days, thank god, because my wife set that up years ago after I missed the water bill twice in one summer. But other things? Absolutely. \n\nI mentioned that youth soccer league earlier, right? The one my nephew plays in? Well, since I’m supposed to be coordinating things, I get a lot of calls from the parent volunteers. And I have just been awful lately about returning those calls. I’ll be driving home from the clinic, I’ll listen to a voicemail from one of the dads asking about the referee schedule or field permits, and I’ll tell myself, 'Okay, I will call him back the exact second I walk through the front door and put my keys down.' But then I walk in, the dog needs to go out, or my wife asks me a question about dinner, and that phone call completely evaporates from my brain. I won't remember until three days later when the guy calls me again, and I have to do this whole song and dance apologizing for leaving him hanging. \n\nAnd it's not just the calls, it's the little errands for these events, too. Like, we had a fundraiser car wash for the league a couple of weeks ago, and my *one* job on the way there was to swing by the hardware store and pick up three extra packs of those microfiber sponges and some more soap. I drove right past the hardware store. Just completely missed it, showed up to the parking lot empty-handed, and had to turn right back around while they were waiting on me. It's so frustrating.\n\nAnd meetings... man, the meetings. I’m on this community advisory board for our neighborhood, and it’s usually the first Thursday of the month. You’d think I’d know that by now, I've been doing it for a year. But once or twice recently, I’ve been sitting at home in my armchair, probably reading one of those Civil War books I told you about, totally relaxed, and my phone buzzes. I look down, and it’s a text from the board president saying, 'Hey, are you joining the meeting? We're waiting on you for quorum.' And my stomach just drops. The community meeting completely slipped my mind. Just vanished. I have to scramble to open my laptop and jump in, and I spend the first ten minutes profusely apologizing to everyone. \n\nIt’s just... it's exhausting feeling like I constantly need a babysitter for my own schedule outside of work, you know? Because at the clinic, I have my calendar open on two different screens all day, but the second I step out of that environment, it's like all my everyday obligations just fall through a sieve.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying excessively about different parts of your life, even when there isn’t an immediate problem?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s funny you ask that, because you’d think with all the balls I’m dropping lately, I’d be a nervous wreck, but... no, not really. I mean, don't get me wrong, I get incredibly frustrated with myself *after* I mess up a schedule or completely miss a community meeting, like I was just saying. That bothers me a lot, and I beat myself up over it because I hate looking incompetent. But just sitting around worrying about things out of the blue? Anticipating disasters when everything is fine? No, that’s just not really how I'm wired. \n\nMy wife, actually, she’s the worrier in the family. She’ll spend hours stressing over whether the roof is going to leak if the forecast calls for a heavy storm, or if the dog is acting slightly more tired than usual, or if our retirement accounts are allocated exactly right for our age bracket... all that stuff. She's always thinking five steps ahead to what *could* go wrong. But me? I just kind of take things as they come. \n\nEven at the clinic—and let me tell you, managing a healthcare facility is basically just putting out one fire after another all day long. A provider calls in sick at 7 AM, the EMR system goes down right when we have a full waiting room, the AC breaks in the middle of July... it's constant chaos. But I don't sit at home on a Sunday night sweating about what *might* break on Monday. I just... I don't know, I just deal with it when it actually happens. Honestly, if I worried about every little thing that could go wrong in a clinic, I'd probably have an ulcer by now. \n\nSo, yeah, I definitely get mad at myself when I make those stupid, careless mistakes, but I don't really have that constantly spinning wheel of worry about life in general or hypothetical problems. I just kind of exist in the moment, for better or worse. Sometimes my wife actually gets annoyed and says I probably *should* worry a little more, especially when I forget to do things she asked me to do, but I just don't have that anxious gear in my brain. If there's no immediate fire to put out, I'm usually pretty relaxed.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of weeks, have you been feeling down, depressed, or hopeless?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, no. I know I just spent all this time complaining about how mad I get at myself when I drop the ball on the soccer league stuff or miss a community meeting, but feeling actually depressed or hopeless? No, not at all. It's really more of an intense annoyance, you know? Like a... well, like a fly buzzing around your head that you just can't quite swat. But it doesn't drag my overall mood down into the gutter or anything like that. \n\nI'm actually a pretty upbeat guy by nature. Even with all the absolute chaos at the clinic—and believe me, there are days where the phones are ringing off the hook, three medical assistants call out sick, and I just want to lock myself in the sterile supply closet and hide—I don't go home feeling like the world is ending or that things are hopeless. I just leave it at the door. I still really enjoy my evenings, I still look forward to the weekends. Like, just this past weekend, my wife and I finally went to this little Italian place downtown that we've been trying to get a reservation at for literally three months, and we had a fantastic time. \n\nI feel like if I were depressed, I probably wouldn't care about stuff like that, right? I'd probably just want to lay in bed or sit in the dark. But I don't have that feeling whatsoever. I have plenty of energy, I still want to go out and do things, I still get totally excited to crack open a new 800-page biography and read for the evening. So, yeah, it's really just the frustration of not being able to keep my ducks in a row when it comes to the little details. Sometimes people at work will see me frantically digging through my desk and they'll ask, 'Oh, you look so overwhelmed, are you okay?' and I have to explain that I'm just trying to remember where I put the updated vacation request forms, not that I'm having some sort of existential crisis. So, no, definitely no hopelessness or feeling down. Just... a lot of scattered moments, I guess you could say.\n\nDoctor: As a child, were you often fidgety or unable to stay seated when you were expected to sit still?\n\nPatient: You know, it is funny because people always seem to assume that if you have trouble focusing, you must have been one of those kids bouncing off the walls, but that was not me at all. Not even a little bit. I remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Higgins... or wait, was it Mrs. Gable? No, Mrs. Gable was fifth grade, Mrs. Higgins was fourth. Anyway, Mrs. Higgins used to tell my parents at parent-teacher conferences that I was a perfect angel because I was so quiet. I was never the kid jumping out of my seat to sharpen my pencil five times a minute or poking the kid in front of me. I was practically glued to my chair. \n\nBut the thing is, I was just completely in my own world. I would be staring out the window at the playground, or I would have a book hidden inside my desk, usually something about history or airplanes, and I would just sit there for an hour without moving a single muscle. So physically, yeah, I was perfectly still. I never got in trouble for running around or being disruptive or not staying in my seat when I was supposed to. I was just... mentally miles and miles away. \n\nEven now, I am really not a big fidgeter. I can sit in those long, incredibly boring budget meetings at the clinic for two hours without tapping my foot or needing to get up and pace around. I mean, I might not hear a single word the finance director says because I am thinking about whether I need to rotate the tires on my truck or what chapter I am on in my book, but physically, I look like I am paying perfect attention. So, yeah, as a kid, staying seated was never the issue. It was what my brain was doing while I was seated that was the problem, if that makes sense. I was just a really quiet, spacey kid, definitely not a hyper one.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you often blurt out answers before the teacher had finished asking the question?\n\nPatient: No, definitely not. Honestly, half the time I didn't even realize the teacher was asking a question in the first place, let alone what the question actually was, so I definitely wasn't the one blurting out the answer! Like I was just saying about Mrs. Higgins' class, or really any of my classes back then, I was so absorbed in whatever else was going on in my own head. \n\nI remember there was this one kid in my grade, Tommy... or maybe it was Timmy? No, Tommy, I'm pretty sure. Anyway, he was always the one shooting his hand up, just shouting out the answer before the teacher even got halfway through her sentence. He was always getting reprimanded for it, you know, 'Raise your hand, Tommy, wait to be called on.' But me? I was usually staring at the wall clock, trying to figure out the exact gear ratio of the second hand, or daydreaming about what I was going to build with my Erector set when I got home. You can't really blurt out an answer if you aren't tracking the conversation, right? \n\nAnd even if I did happen to be paying attention for a minute, which was pretty rare, I was way too reserved to just shout something across the room. I'd probably sit there second-guessing if I even had the right answer anyway, thinking, 'Well, maybe it's this, but what if she means something else?' By the time I'd even consider raising my hand, they had already moved on to the next subject entirely. So no, blurting things out, interrupting the teacher... that just wasn't me at all. I was practically the invisible kid in the back row.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I was practically the invisible kid in the back row."
  }

Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nHi, doctor. Yeah, it's nice to meet you too. Well, I finally decided to make an appointment because things at work have just been getting... well, I guess they've always been a bit of a struggle in this one specific area, but it's just becoming too much to ignore lately. I'm a health services manager, so I oversee operations for a few different medical clinics, and it's a good job, I've been doing it a while, but... \n\nThe main reason I’m here is that anything that takes real sitting-down brain work feels like I just hit a massive brick wall. Like, if I have to do the monthly compliance reports, or if I'm trying to plan out a multi-site health event—which I have to do a couple of times a year—it requires looking at spreadsheets, budgets, coordinating schedules... and the second I sit at my desk to actually do it, my brain just completely resists. I keep putting it off, over and over, even when I know it really matters. \n\nAnd that's the frustrating part, you know? I know how important it is. If those reports aren't filed, we could get cited, and it's a huge headache for the directors. But instead of just doing the heavy mental lifting, I'll find fifty other things to do around the clinic, like checking the inventory or talking to the floor staff, just to avoid sitting at that desk. I'll tell myself I'll sit down and do the report at noon, and then noon comes and I just avoid it again. I even bring my laptop home and just stare at it on the kitchen island for hours without getting anywhere. It's just... forcing myself to do that deep, sustained thinking is exhausting before I even type a single word, and I'm just tired of fighting myself on it every single month.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. I'd just be staring out the window, watching the playground, or looking at the clock, completely lost in my own world instead of focusing on the lessons in front of me. \n\nAnd the schoolwork itself, man, it was a total disaster zone. I was constantly losing my worksheets. The teacher would hand them out, and by the time I got home, they were just gone, vanished into thin air. It would just sit crumpled at the bottom of my bag. It got to the point where my mother had to institute this whole strict nightly routine because she was so frustrated. She literally had to physically check my backpack every single night. She would pull everything out, smooth out the crumpled papers, pack it all back up, and remind me exactly what to turn in. If she didn't do that, if she missed even one night of checking my bag, absolutely nothing would make it to the teacher's desk. Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYou know, it's funny you ask that, because people always assume that if you're not paying attention, it's because you're bored or you don't care about them, but honestly... no, I don't really have that problem. Like, if I'm sitting down with one of my clinic directors, or if I'm having a one-on-one with a staff member who's upset about their schedule, I'm actually really locked in. I can look right at them and hear exactly what they're saying. I don't find myself zoning out in the middle of a conversation or having to ask people to repeat themselves because my mind went somewhere else. \n\nI mean, sure, if it's a massive, two-hour all-staff meeting where someone is just droning on at the front of the room with a PowerPoint, my mind might wander a bit, but I feel like that's pretty normal for anyone, right? But if it's a direct conversation, just me and another person, face-to-face? No, I'm right there with them. I don't drift off. It's really just the paperwork and the solo, heavy-thinking tasks that completely derail me. When it comes to actually talking to people and listening to them, I feel like I'm completely present. I've never had a spouse or a boss get mad at me for spacing out while they were talking to my face, if that makes sense. So, yeah, I wouldn't say that's an issue for me at all.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.
